‘Feminism Is the Consequence of the “Man Problem,” Not Its Cause’
2026-09-15

‘Feminism Is the Consequence of the “Man Problem,” Not Its Cause’

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. Helen Lewis’s “The Men Who Don’t Want Women to Vote” is the most important thing I have read this year. My husband has said that Kamala Harris lost the 2024 election because of the misogyny that exists in America; Lewis’s article proved him correct, with just a teaspoon of humor mixed into an otherwise very frightening topic. Upon finishing “The Men Who Don’t Want Women to Vote,” I found myself equal parts stunned and galvanized. Lewis has an extraordinary ability to illuminate what so many of us feel but struggle to articulate, and this article was no exception. The MAGA movement contends that feminism is to blame for American decline. But feminism didn’t emerge from nothing. Feminism is the consequence of the “man problem” Lewis identifies, not its cause. The Bible passage that anti-feminist commentators love to cite is from Ephesians: “Wives, submit to your husbands.” But that’s where they stop reading. The verse goes on to say that a husband must provide for his wife, and love her as he loves his own body. This is not a “do as I say or else” arrangement; it is a covenant of mutual care—her health and happiness are meant to be as important to him as his own, and vice versa. When men forget the instruction to care for their wives as they care for themselves, women have no choice but to learn to care for themselves. I was recently at the pharmacy, and the man ahead of me—trying to pick up a prescription for his sick wife—answered the pharmacist’s questions with “I don’t know; my wife takes care of that.” When a husband utters, “My wife takes care of that,” the word that means “everything.” It means knowing the passwords, the medications and dosages, the appointment schedules. It means knowing what to say to assuage a teenager’s broken heart, whom to call at the school, how to manage a crisis. It means caring for sick husbands and aging parents—sacrificing for the people you love. I am the breadwinner in my family. My husband stayed home and raised our three daughters. The plan was for him to return to work once the girls were in elementary school. But we all know what happens when we make plans—God laughs. My husband now lives with a neurological condition that complicates our lives in ways neither of us anticipated. Does working to provide health care for my children and husband make me less of a woman? Would my husband be mocked in the manosphere for not working? Is staying home to raise children noble only when a woman does it? I worry that “The Men Who Don’t Want Women to Vote” misses a crucial component of the rise of masculinism. I stand in vocal opposition to misogyny and the idiocy of the far-right masculine media sphere. I teach my daughters that they control their bodies and that they should never miss a vote. But I do think that a large cohort of men is struggling with issues that have been ignored, or sometimes even mocked, by the mainstream media. Many men try to be good husbands and parents, only to hear mantras like “We don’t need men” and “There are no good men out there”; on social media, people discuss whether they’d prefer to be trapped in the woods with a man or a bear. Meanwhile, American men are nearly four times more likely than women to die by suicide. The manosphere discusses that issue frequently, as Lewis notes; the mainstream media do not. When men hear negative messages about being a man from the mainstream media but see the manosphere addressing their struggles, they will gravitate toward it. I hope society can learn to address male issues without mocking them. Otherwise, I fear, the extremism will continue. A significant number of men are never fully socialized. These men have few if any friends—and certainly no female friends. Deep down, they may not hate or fear women; they just don’t know how to interact with them. They never learned how to work with or cooperate with them. If one accepts that, then quixotic attempts to repeal women’s suffrage or strip women of social and political freedoms should not surprise anyone. Helen Lewis makes a convincing argument that the noisy, often lewd misogyny of the MAGA right is no longer fringe and presents a real danger to women. This sexist worldview reflects and interacts with patriarchal systems across the world. Examining more than 350 variables across 176 nations, the scholars Valerie M. Hudson, Donna Lee Bowen, and Perpetua Lynne Nielsen have demonstrated that “the fate of nations is tied to the status of women.” Their 2020 book, The First Political Order: How Sex Shapes Governance and National Security Worldwide, shows that nations that subordinate women tend to be more fragile, autocratic, corrupt, and impoverished. The subordination of women appears to produce the kind of governance that leads to worse outcomes for everyone. The sexual political order is, as they write, the “first political order”—what starts in a family shapes a broader community’s politics. As you might imagine, this article prompted a large and varied mailbag. I heard from grandfathers—happily married for decades—who couldn’t understand how society seemed to have gone backwards, from middle-aged men who felt overlooked because their problems weren’t fashionable, and from parents who worried about what their sons were watching online. I came away thinking that screen-time limits are good, that schools should do more to accommodate rambunctious boys as well as conscientious girls, and that the Democrats need to develop their own, positive visions of masculinity to compete with those of Andrew Tate and Nick Fuentes. Some of the likely 2028 presidential contenders have started to do that: Gavin Newsom talks regularly about men’s problems, and signed an executive order aimed at improving mental-health services and reducing male suicide rates in California. Pete Buttigieg has learned how to bro out on podcasts such as Andrew Schulz’s Flagrant—the kind of spaces where Donald Trump did so well during the 2024 campaign. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez waded into the debate over the looks-maxxer Clavicular (the guy who claims to have achieved a better jawline by hitting himself with hammers) by highlighting the pressures on young men to conform to unrealistic beauty standards. The conversation is definitely shifting. In this month’s cover story, “Empire of Grift,” Anne Applebaum examines how hardened ideologues and opportunistic profiteers are bending American diplomacy to advance an antidemocratic agenda—and their own financial interests. For our cover, the illustrator Ben Hickey created an ominous image of figures in shadow arrayed around a map of the world, evoking the war-room scenes from Stanley Kubrick’s 1964 film, Dr. Strangelove or: How I Learned to Stop Worrying and Love the Bomb. “The Great Romance Slump” (September) stated that birth control was legalized for married couples in 1965. In fact, in 1965 the Supreme Court held that the government couldn’t restrict a married couple’s access to birth control. “Confessions of a Token Black Professor” (September) described a sign affixed to the wall of a former mill in Lewiston, Maine, as neon. In fact, it is a marquee sign. This article appears in the October 2026 print edition with the headline “The Commons.”

Another option I wanted to propose (but got cut short) is to offer access to our internal model so that they could try to finish their proof and bridge the gap between Euler and NS. Again I did not know how to navigate giving access to internal OpenAI IP to an Anthropic employee. 3) To reiterate it plainly: as my text clearly indicates, and as I said during our call, OpenAI's intention was to do everything possible to celebrate their mathematical achievements and the heroic efforts that they made on Euler. In the call I was immediately met with a litany of slander, including direct threats that if we were to announce Navier-Stokes he would immediately go to the press with a barrage of unfounded accusations. I refuted all these accusations but he replied “there is nothing you can do, I simply do not trust you”. I was confused why one would turn an incredible source for celebration (of their achievements!) into such bickering, which is when I said that I did not understand why one would risk their career [over unfounded accusations]. Genuinely, at that moment, I was trying to care for him and do a last ditch attempt to get a chance to give them all the credits that they deserve. I deeply apologize for this extremely poor choice of words, it is the opposite of what I was trying to convey. I don't know about any particular phone, but in many devices the long press power off is an old-fashioned kill switch that charges a capacitor, eventually reaching the voltage to turn off a transistor connected to a "power good" line on the power management IC. And inside the IC it's the same deal. So as long as the button works, it should be able to hard power off, even if the software is badly bugged. Actually numerous times I've seen people complaining their phone or tablet wouldn't turn off, when actually the software was just frozen and they didn't know about long press power off. "May I see that?" "Wow... that's amazing!" "To you, maybe..." Not necessarily a capacitor; sometimes a little counter circuit, on the MCU but independent of the CPU and not controllable (or vetoable) by it. In either case, not prone to systemic failures of the higher-level logic. People overestimate the difficulty of adding low-level contingencies to high-level systems, and underestimate engineers' eagerness to do so. But this is the sort of thing that EEs worry about all the time. When I'm worried my phone doesn't turn itself off, I just use a small flyswatter. (But it's expensive! I really ought to try this long press thing one day.) We bought some Dell towers last year where even the long press of the power button can apparently be overridden by software. Well, I ended up holding that button down for more than 60 seconds withou Yup, that was actually was I was going to do next. Funny thing, though. After I eventually let go of the power button (it may have even been 2 minutes that I'd kept it depressed) with the intent of physically unplugging that box - the progress bar finally started moving, and the machine installed the various updates which it had staged. The Luna experiment illustrates an important distinction between task automation and organizational autonomy. An agent may be capable of interacting with email, browsers, telephones, financial systems, and other operational tools, yet still lack the higher-level judgment required to operate a viable business. Retail, for example, requires continuous decisions about demand, inventory, pricing, customer behavior, risk, and capital allocation. These are not simply interface problems. The vending-machine incident demonstrates a related issue: increasing an agent's operational authority also increases the potential consequences of poorly specified objectives, adversarial inputs, and unforeseen edge cases. Giving an autonomous system access to financial resources therefore changes the problem from "Can the agent perform the task?" to "Can the agent reliably determine which actions should be performed?" That makes Pion an interesting research direction. A platform that enables many independent experiments could provide considerably more evidence about the practical limits of autonomous agents than a small number of carefully controlled demonstrations. The ultimate measure of success, however, should not be whether an agent can technically operate a company. It should be whether the resulting organization can make sound decisions, remain economically viable, respond appropriately to unexpected conditions, and produce value for actual customers. In other words, autonomy is not merely the ability to act without a human in the loop. It is the ability to exercise useful judgment when the correct action is not already specified. In other words, autonomy is not merely the ability to act without a human in the loop. Despite the Supreme Court’s Ruling, Trump’s Still Trying to Tip the Midterms
2026-09-15

Despite the Supreme Court’s Ruling, Trump’s Still Trying to Tip the Midterms

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. On Monday, the Supreme Court blocked Donald Trump’s effort to throw mail balloting into chaos ahead of the November midterm elections. But that was just one prong of Trump’s attempts to tilt races toward Republicans. Today we’ll explain the others. This Supreme Court ruling seems like a big deal. What exactly happened? You aren’t wrong. As the legal expert Richard Hasen explains in Slate, Trump’s executive order would have created onerous new requirements for mail-in voting that risked disenfranchising millions of elderly, military, and other voters. Seven of nine justices apparently saw that potential catastrophe coming and rejected it, prompting a Tuesday-morning tantrum from the president. Trump has repeatedly tried to get the court to take his side on mail-in ballots, but this ruling looks like the end of the line—at least for this year’s elections. OK, bullet dodged. But what else is Trump doing that we should worry about? Trump’s order was backed by his unfounded claims that mail voting is fraudulent, part of his broader obsession with the false notion that Democrats are “cheating” to win elections. Trump has said he wants to “take over” elections, which are currently administered by state-level officials. That decentralized system is actually a bulwark against the kind of cheating Trump claims to care about—and against a sore-loser president trying to change election rules to protect his own interests. Still, Trump’s administration is trying to get states to hand over voter data and hunting through the rolls for alleged fraud. It’s true that individuals have sometimes illegally cast ballots. But many of those cases have been accidents, like when a software error in New Jersey improperly registered noncitizens in 2023 and 2024, and widespread fraud is a myth. Per the Heritage Foundation, a conservative think tank that helped write Project 2025, there have been about 100 cases of noncitizen voting over the past 25 years. Nevertheless, the administration has asked almost every state to turn over databases that include voters’ personal information. Most have resisted, sparking court battles that the administration has overwhelmingly lost. The Department of Homeland Security, meanwhile, has assigned hundreds of employees to parse public voting databases for noncitizens. Some have allegedly posed as voters, potentially violating state privacy laws. Yet Secretary Markwayne Mullin has publicly touted unverified numbers of alleged noncitizen voters and threatened election officials with prison time. But surely those efforts can be contested in court? Democrats and voting-rights groups have already sued over some of Trump’s moves (hence the case rejecting his mail ballot order). But some fear the administration is looking for ways to circumvent courts. Late last month, a whistleblower alleged that the U.S. Postal Service had continued to build an online portal that could’ve thrown out mail ballots en masse despite an order blocking work on it. (The Postal Service claims it hasn’t defied any court orders.) Courts can’t stop Trump from sowing doubt about the soundness of American elections. He has ordered investigations into debunked voting irregularities in states he lost in 2020 and continues to attack voting machines despite there being no evidence that they’ve ever changed election results. That could lay the groundwork to claim there was fraud if certain races don’t go Republicans’ way this fall. And even if judges toss those claims, they may still depress faith in the results. One recent survey found that less than half of Americans expect the midterms to be free and fair, with most Republicans doubting that mailed ballots are counted accurately and most Democrats fearful that Trump will deploy immigration agents to polling places. So, while the voting could ultimately go off without a hitch, the crisis of confidence continues. The midterms are still several weeks away, so let’s turn to a more urgent question: How to enjoy the rest of your evening. My colleagues have some ideas that don’t require an appeal to the Supreme Court. Take a sip: Martinis are savored for their elegant simplicity and refined romanticism. Now their iconic imagery has been repurposed as chintzy merch and wine mom–style decor. Courtney Iseman asks, “When did we as a society collectively decide, We martini, therefore we are?” Reconsider your “smart” home: Cameras are becoming more and more common in home appliances, including in smart ovens, refrigerators, doorbells, and toothbrushes—even as people decry surveillance measures in public. What does this trend mean for our consumption? Steffi Cao has a prediction. Give your brain a workout: What’s an 11-letter “Enya hit with the repeated lyric ‘Sail away, sail away, sail away’ ”? What’s a nine-letter “cutesy term for spooky snakes”? Sharpen your wordplay skills with today’s crossword. Revisit articles by Slate alums Daniel Engber, David Plotz, Emily Yoffe, and more. We’ll be updating this page over the next month, so keep an eye out for more pieces from the vault! Slate was one of the first news outlets I read regularly, so I’m really enjoying the birthday throwback. Happy trails back to 1996, and hope you have a great night! Trumpworld Has Found Its New Jeffrey Epstein
2026-09-15

Trumpworld Has Found Its New Jeffrey Epstein

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. The specter of Jeffrey Epstein continues to haunt the Trump administration seven years after the sexual predator’s death. But the makings of the next Epstein-style scandal—one that ultimately could be even more damaging for Trump and MAGAworld—appears to be unfolding right now. The Tate brothers, Andrew and Tristan, are alleged to be sex predators on a level approaching Epstein and with perhaps even more political clout with Donald Trump. For those who are blessedly unfamiliar with the Tates, they are highly popular “manosphere” influencers who have garnered an international following, particularly among young men, for their particular brand of content focused on their prideful misogyny and flashing of their apparent wealth. They are also accused of running a decadelong international sex-trafficking ring, with credible allegations against them in multiple countries from dozens of accusers involving gruesome acts of sexual violence, abuse, and exploitation against women and minors. And they have many connections to the world’s top political elite, including Donald Trump’s family members and close advisers. Sound familiar? Just like Epstein, the Tates have seemingly leveraged these connections to receive favorable treatment and avoid the full extent of justice. After years of investigations and attempted prosecutions, the Tates find themselves in the crosshairs yet again. In July, they were charged in Britain on rape charges that also included allegations of possession of child sexual abuse imagery—and are currently sitting in a jail in Miami, fighting extradition. And earlier this month, Romanian prosecutors indicted Andrew for trafficking minors, money laundering, sex with a minor, and witness tampering, and Tristan with complicity. But, in a few important ways, the involvement of Trump’s administration and allies with the Tates could be even more egregious than the Epstein controversy. While the Epstein scandal has centered on holding bad actors accountable for past actions and current cover-ups, the Tate brothers’ scandal is a live issue, and there is reason to believe that if the Trump administration buckles in their favor, the brothers could go back to their criminal activities. Unlike Epstein, who generally lurked in the shadows of elite circles, the Tates brazenly and publicly broadcast their depraved behavior for years. The Tates established their popularity by building online and porn businesses, and becoming internet sensations through their aggressive and controversial misogynistic opinions. Indicia of their alleged criminality have been publicly available for years, including Andrew attacking a woman on camera in 2016. In the process, they lured in young women and allegedly sexually abused and raped them. The victims allegedly include minors. Accusations and law enforcement investigations of rape and strangulation have been swirling around the Tates since at least 2015, when they relocated from the U.K. to Romania to escape possible arrest. Rape allegations persisted in the subsequent years, as the Tates continued to grow their online following. In 2023, the Tates and several co-conspirators were formally charged by Romanian authorities, with allegations from about 35 alleged victims, including minors. The Tate brothers were barred from leaving Romania while the prosecution continued, having been labeled flight risks and public dangers. (The Tate brothers deny all allegations of criminal conduct.) Despite this, their following grew. And, just as Epstein used his wealth and social status to develop political relationships, the Tate brothers’ rise in popularity has come with an increase in political influence, which they have eagerly flaunted. The connections between the Tates and Trumpworld multiplied over time, even as accusations against them piled up. They developed a relationship with the president’s youngest son, Barron, who encouraged his father to engage with the manosphere podcasts and ecosystem. They were personally in touch with Donald Trump Jr., who livestreamed with Andrew in 2024 and called the Tates’ Romanian detention in 2023 “absolute insanity.” (The White House has not responded to questions about Barron’s relationship with the Tates.) After the 2024 election, shows of support from top Trump advisers became even louder. Vice President–elect J.D. Vance followed the Tates on X; right before Trump’s inauguration, incoming counselor to the president Alina Habba said she was a “big fan” of the Tates, compared their legal issues to Trump’s, and told Andrew, “If you’re in the States, I got your back.” After Trump took office, one of the Tates’ former lawyers, Paul Ingrassia, became the White House’s liaison to the Justice Department and then the Department of Homeland Security. These connections appeared to pay off almost immediately after Trump’s inauguration. In February 2025, Richard Grenell, the Trump administration’s special envoy, attended the Munich Security Conference with Vice President Vance and seemed to push for the Tates’ release. Vance’s speech to the conference, in part, echoed right-wing critiques of a recent Romanian judicial decision. Meanwhile, Grenell, after being lobbied by the Tates’ PR team to help the brothers, held a side meeting with Romanian officials at the conference in which he reportedly asked how “the Tate boys” were doing. The New Yorker reported that it seemed clear to the Romanian foreign minister that the Americans were supporting the Tates because the Tates backed Trump in 2024, and that the Tates were “playing a role within the bilateral relations” between the two countries. (The White House denied this, and Grenell denied helping the Tates, but has publicly stated: “I support the Tate brothers.”) Nevertheless, Grenell’s and Vance’s behavior appears to have been interpreted as subtle but clear pressure from the Trump administration to release the Tates. Which is precisely what happened days later. Romania lifted the travel restrictions, in the face of fierce opposition by prosecutors, and the brothers gallivanted to meet their MAGA friends in the United States. When they arrived in Miami, Trump’s oldest living political svengali Roger Stone posted a picture with them and wrote: “Richard Grenell secured the release of the Tates.” According to the New Yorker, Andrew “denied that Donald Trump had personally intervened, but said he had learned from powerful people in Bucharest that the restrictions were removed because of American pressure.” With the Tates now finding themselves in law enforcement’s dragnet yet again, the parallels with Epstein are striking. Although the Department of Justice arrested the Tates in July 2026, assisting the U.K.’s extradition efforts under the new criminal charges, and the White House said they wouldn’t get involved in the matter, the brothers are continuing to try to use their influence to pressure the Trump administration. Following the arrest, the Tates’ lawyer said: “Andrew Tate was a material participant in ensuring President Trump’s victory. And if Trump doesn’t support him, and if the next people who are going to be running for office potentially are just like, yeah, down the river he goes, that young cohort of people will break with the Republican Party in the next election.” As the Epstein scandal has taught us, the fact that Trump’s DOJ arrested the Tates does not lessen the severity of what has already transpired, or the risks going forward. The last time the Tates wriggled away from custody and came to the U.S., a woman in California accused Andrew of choking her and battering her head and face; after their encounter, she was diagnosed with post-concussion syndrome. L.A. prosecutors did not ultimately bring charges but she is pursuing a lawsuit against him, including as evidence her medical report and texts in which he threatened to beat her. The same dynamic transpired with Epstein two decades ago: He, too, was arrested and charged but continued to put significant pressure on the government and ultimately received a sweetheart deal. He then went back to his life of luxury and sex crimes. We’ve already seen the Trump administration take aggressive steps to help supporters who have been charged with crimes and convicted, so his administration’s possible involvement in the Tate brothers’ story isn’t necessarily over. The Tates are continuing to challenge their extradition, and ultimately, once they exhaust their legal options, whether they are extradited to face their charges or not would ultimately be up to the Trump administration. The scope of Epstein’s criminal behavior, and the full extent of his social network, was largely ignored while he was at large. Today, the Tates and the apparent depths of their interlocking connections to people around Trump have broadly fallen under the radar of mainstream media coverage. But for younger Americans they are totemic figures in this political and cultural moment, and their influence is particularly pernicious. That’s why there must be full transparency and congressional investigations to unravel what has already happened and expose the full extent of their relationship with Trumpworld, and also to uncover what, if any, efforts are continuing to be made now by their allies to pressure the Trump administration as the Tates continue to face charges in the United Kingdom and Romania. As a member of the legal team representing the victims of Jeffrey Epstein in litigation, I can attest that the struggle to achieve justice after his death has been righteous but hard. The Tates must be held accountable for their actions and neither favor nor fame can be allowed to shield them. The Epstein scandal and what happened to his victims is heinous; the Trump administration had begun to repeat that miscarriage of justice. Hopefully, they won’t be allowed to continue. The Supreme Court’s Conservatives and Liberals Just Came to the Brink—and Finally Said No
2026-09-15

The Supreme Court’s Conservatives and Liberals Just Came to the Brink—and Finally Said No

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. There are two ways of looking at the Supreme Court’s three-sentence ruling in USPS v. California Monday evening, which put an end to Donald Trump’s attempt to disenfranchise millions of elderly, disabled, military, rural, Native American, and other voters in the 2026 midterm elections who depend upon vote by mail to cast a ballot. The first is to lament that the government pushed this case to its limits—going not once, not twice, but three times to the high court—and that two justices, Samuel Alito and Clarence Thomas, were willing to simply ignore mass disenfranchisement and side with Trump’s bogus voter-fraud crusade. The second is to note that the court’s conservatives and liberals came to the brink, saw that ruling the other way risked serious election subversion, and said no. For the time being, let’s celebrate that the center of the court did hold, even as dangers remain ahead in both 2026 and 2028. Slate readers are already familiar with this long saga, so just a brief review is necessary. Back in March, Trump put out an executive order on voting (his second since returning to office in 2025) that, among other things, directed the Postal Service to establish a rule imposing a new set of requirements for states that aimed to continue having their ballots delivered by mail. The rule, issued in late August, mandated that states receive USPS approval for new envelope designs with intelligent bar codes on them, print those bar codes on each envelope, upload a list of their voters to a USPS portal, and submit the ballots; the agency would then check the names encoded in the bar codes against those in the states’ voter lists. The rule further stipulated that USPS should reject entire batches of ballots if there was one potential mismatch. During the brief time when courts had not yet blocked the rule from taking effect, USPS rejected the ballot design of Leon County, Florida, because its required printed notation about return mail was 0.014 of an inch too close to other information on the envelope. Now, complying with all these new regulations would be pretty challenging for election administrators to do, even with lead time and practice. But the government tried to impose the rules for ongoing voting. Envelopes have already been loaded with ballots and addressed. Indeed, North Carolina began mailing ballots on Sept. 4, and other states are doing so now. Even as of Monday’s SCOTUS ruling, USPS did not have a working portal for states to upload their voter lists, not even on a voluntary basis, something the agency had said would be possible by last week. The upshot of USPS’s administrative failure is that the rule would have stopped the agency from accepting ballots from all states because there could be no matching between the envelope and the portal. That means that, across the country, the most vulnerable voters who depend on vote by mail would have been disenfranchised, including core Republican constituencies. The federal district court issued a preliminary injunction partly on this basis, noting the burdens on election administrators and the public interest costs. The court also found that the rule exceeded USPS’s authority to issue. The Supreme Court’s terse order agreed on both points. It held that the government was likely to lose on the legal question of USPS’s authority. And it held that the “equitable factors”—that is, the burden on election administrators and the voting public—outweighed the government’s purported interest in stopping voter fraud. White-hot fury that the government tried so hard to get this rule in place for the 2026 elections, knowing full well the consequences had it been successful at the Supreme Court, is the correct emotion. A good legal brief takes on the strongest argument of the other side and deals with it head-on. Here, the government had to overcome the district court’s factual findings about the unfairness of the rule. But the government’s brief simply ignored the mass disenfranchisement point, one flagged by the states and by plaintiffs from voting rights organizations, as well as in an amicus brief written by professors, a document that the Safeguarding Democracy Project filed in this case. The failure to address the mass disenfranchisement point essentially conceded it, and that was enough for the court majority. But it was not enough for Alito, dissenting along with Thomas. Alito first concluded that the U.S. was likely to win on the merits. When he turned to the equities, he parroted the empty and unsupported claim that the USPS guidelines would stop fraud. On the other side of the ledger, he asserted that states were divided on the equities because some Trump-supporting state attorneys general had filed a brief siding with the government; never mind that some of those states’ chief election officers filed on the other side. In any event, he went on to blame the states for litigating the case so hard that it somehow stopped the government from getting its rule and portal up and running; he also, once again, implicitly blamed Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson for slow-walking the case when it first came before the justices in July. Totally absent from Alito’s balancing were the voters. They weren’t an afterthought; their interests were not considered at all. This is unsurprising, considering that the justice has done more to hurt the cause of voting and voting rights than anyone else on the court in at least a century. So there are good reasons to be furious and worried about what this portends. But let’s not be all doom and gloom. We know that Justice Brett Kavanaugh, who agreed, in a separate concurrence, that the government might win on the merits, saw that the equities favored the voters this time around. And we can be sure that at least two of the court’s other conservatives agreed too, along with the court’s three liberals, on both the merits and the equities. A third conservative justice either agreed with the majority or did not see fit to side with Alito’s dissent or Kavanaugh’s concurrence. That ain’t nothing. Just as in the period after the 2020 presidential election, when Trump tried to subvert the outcome with bogus legal arguments and conspiracy theories, the Supreme Court again stood firm and said no. Back then, it rejected without comment an original petition from Texas AG Ken Paxton to throw out the results from some states that had voted for President Joe Biden. Now it has failed to countenance a mass disenfranchisement just weeks before a critical midterm. The Supreme Court has been terrible on voting rights, but so far it has held the line on actual election rigging. Let’s celebrate that, even as we gird for more battles to come. Trump Wants to Prevent Future Presidents From Fighting Climate Change
2026-09-14

Trump Wants to Prevent Future Presidents From Fighting Climate Change

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. It’s a great day for people who hate the idea of a livable planet. For everyone else, not so much. The Trump administration today plans to both rescind existing rules on climate pollution from power plants and undercut future administrations’ ability to regulate those emissions. These moves face court challenges. But if they go through, they would gut what’s left of U.S. climate policy and undermine efforts to stave off the worst effects of climate change. What do the new Trump rules actually say? Both the Obama and Biden administrations used the Clean Air Act to try to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, which drive climate change. Now Donald Trump, who has labeled climate change a “con job” and twice pulled out of international agreements meant to mitigate it, is reversing those efforts. One of his new rules would reportedly eliminate Biden-era regulations that required power plants that run on coal and natural gas to limit their carbon emissions. If that rule matches a version reporters obtained last year, it will argue that power plants don’t contribute significantly to pollution or climate change because they’re “a small and decreasing part of global emissions,” even though coal remains the worst-polluting fossil fuel. The second proposal would be even more far-reaching. It’s expected to claim that power plant emissions in general don’t meet the Clean Air Act’s standard for Environmental Protection Agency regulation. The power industry is, behind transportation, the second-largest source of planet-warming emissions in the United States and the second-largest producer of greenhouse gas emissions in the world. Kneecapping the EPA’s ability to regulate the industry under the Clean Air Act would gut one of the main ways Democratic presidents have tried to minimize climate impacts and tie future Democratic presidents’ hands. What else is Trump doing to fuel climate change? Earlier this year, Trump rescinded a long-standing EPA finding that greenhouse gases jeopardize human health. That determination, known as the “endangerment finding,” had been the basis of federal climate regulation for nearly two decades. Without it, the administration has effectively given itself carte blanche to go after all manner of climate regulations. Trump has lifted restrictions on vehicle tailpipe emissions, canceled billions for clean energy projects in blue states, and delayed efforts to eliminate chemicals that are hundreds or thousands of times better at trapping heat than carbon dioxide. How is the administration justifying this? Trump officials argue that climate rules burden the fossil fuel industry, hurt the economy, and increase what Americans pay for electricity. In truth, the EPA’s own assessments recently found that repealing power-plant pollution standards will do little to lower utility bills or save the coal industry, which has been in decline for decades. But it would be a major favor to the GOP’s fossil fuel benefactors, saving the industry billions. The economic impacts for the rest of us will probably be imperceptible, particularly when compared to the damage Trump’s moves threaten to do to the environment and to public health. Surely there’s something that people worried about climate change can do about these new rules? Democratic-led states and environmental organizations will sue over these rules, as they have over the others. Those challenges could take years to resolve. But the Supreme Court seems likely to hear at least some of them, and its conservative majority has shown a blasé attitude toward climate change. In 2016, Chief Justice John Roberts led a behind-the-scenes effort to block the Clean Power Plan, an Obama initiative to slash emissions, in part citing “irreparable harm” to energy companies. In 2022, the court narrowed the EPA’s ability to regulate power plant emissions, forcing Joe Biden to adopt more-piecemeal measures. That doesn’t sound promising. So what would it mean if these rules took effect? Think of the fight against climate change like a tug-of-war, with emissions on one end of the rope and efforts to mitigate them on the other. Trump’s moves would tilt the playing field toward emissions. This means not only larger and faster changes to the climate but also an increase in other health effects due to the pollution that fossil-fuel-burning power plants spew, which can negatively affect anyone with lungs. The Biden administration estimated that its power plant rules would’ve prevented up to 1,200 premature deaths and 360,000 asthma attacks in 2035 alone. Some Democratic-led states have stepped into the breach, pursuing tougher rules of their own and even trying to force fossil fuel companies to pay for damage from wildfires, floods, and other climate-related disasters. Still, states probably can’t balance out a president who’s doing everything in his power to spur climate change along. And time is of the essence. Pondering the potential heat death of humanity is a bummer of a way to start the week, I know. To get your mind off it for the rest of the night, my colleagues suggest: TV worth rewatching: What is it about the HBO miniseries Band of Brothers that keeps viewers coming back for more, 25 years after it originally aired? Slate’s Rebecca Onion investigates why fans are so attached to the show. This game show tradition was Alex Trebek’s favorite: What is Jeopardy!’s annual Teen Tournament? Claire McNear, America’s preeminent Jeopardy! reporter, did a deep dive into the beloved event—which disappeared after Trebek’s death—and got some news about its future. Some snack science: Consumer Reports recently published a scary-sounding investigation on chemicals present in common kids’ snacks. Epidemiologist Gideon Meyerowitz-Katz breaks down the science and concludes that the results are actually pretty reassuring. A three-ingredient treat: If you’d rather nosh on a less processed snack, Noah Lederman recommends a super-fresh dish that’s perfect for late summer: a heavenly concoction he’s dubbed Fish Crudo With Melon and Tomato Water. OK, that’s all from me. Happy munching, and see you tomorrow! Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. Your AI agents' reports and questions have a new inbox, courtesy of AWS
2026-09-15

Your AI agents' reports and questions have a new inbox, courtesy of AWS

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Pizza Bot runs locally and lets users interact with AI agents in an email-like interface. Perfect for blowing them off just like your human colleagues! AWS engineers have published an open-source AI agent management tool that treats tasks and permission requests just like emails that fill up an inbox. Pizza Bot was announced on AWS’ open-source blog last week, where it was billed by its developers as a way to more easily manage agent tasks without having to constantly check a chat window to see if a task was completed or on hold because more permission was needed. “You don’t send an email and then sit watching the outbox until the reply lands,” the Pizza Bot team wrote. “Pizza Bot is shaped like an email client for the same reason: a thread is a unit of work you come back to rather than a session you have to attend.” Like the modern email services it mimics, Pizza Bot organizes communications with AI agents into message threads. Finished tasks, along with detailed result writeups you’d expect from a colleague you’ve delegated your work to, end up in the Unread category, while anything you need to make a decision on is shunted to the Action category. Asynchronous agent communication is likely to be a godsend for those balancing multiple tasks assigned to digital delegees, as the Pizza Bot team describes it. “Live chat assumes both parties are present, which holds for a quick exchange and breaks the moment a task takes several minutes,” the Pizza Bot maintainers wrote. Just like you don’t want to stare over the shoulder of the poor IT sod you sent off to do a task while you watch YouTube videos, no one wants to sit there and watch an AI agent do web research, or whatever. “Pizza Bot treats an agent the way you’d treat a colleague who has gone off to do the work: it comes back when there’s something to read, or something only you can decide,” the PB team added. Pizza Bot, named for Amazon’s “two-pizza team” model (i.e., small, autonomous teams small enough to be fed by two pizzas), began life inside Amazon as an internal tool for AI agent uses other than software development. According to its developers, non-coding uses they were exploring internally continually had the same requirements, namely working in the background autonomously and only bugging their user when they were done or had a question. “None of those describe how anyone uses a chat window,” the Pizza Bot team explained. “They describe how people already work with each other.” Email, they added, was the perfect design paradigm for internal use, and seeing whether it would work for everyone else meant opening the project to open source contributions. As mentioned, Pizza Bot is open source - you can snag a copy for Windows, macOS, and Linux from its GitHub repo. It runs on-device, stores all its communication threads locally in SQLite files, and doesn’t transmit anything off device unless you grant permission for it to do so. Prompts and attachments are sent to model providers, naturally, but even that’s not necessary: Pizza Bot also supports Ollama, allowing users to run compatible open-weight AI models locally. Pizza Bot doesn’t ship with a lot of capabilities, as its maintainers noted that Amazon-specific skills and MCP servers were left out when they rebuilt it as an open source project for public release. Out of the box, however, it can list, read, write, edit, and search files, delegate tasks to specialist agents, and run a sandboxed JavaScript interpreter. It also ships with a browser automation skill and a guide skill that lets users ask about Pizza Bot’s capabilities. Claude Code-compatible .mcp.json files are supported and can be dropped straight in, as can skills, the team noted. One word of caution to anyone who wants to try it: The devs make clear this is a piece of open source software, not an officially supported AWS product. “There’s no AWS support or service-level agreement behind it,” they note. If you’re gonna bake a pie with this bot, you’ve been warned. ® Intel spin-off Cornelis and newcomer Delos Data pitch open alternatives for scaling AI beyond the rack PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Pizza Bot runs locally and lets users interact with AI agents in an email-like interface. Low-quality casino sites conceal highly dangerous threat actors
2026-09-15

Low-quality casino sites conceal highly dangerous threat actors

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Security firm Infoblox shines light on malicious infrastructure lurking beneath illegal gambling sites If your employees are visiting Chinese-language gambling or adult sites, they may not just be wasting time and money, but potentially encountering serious malware hidden behind domains that look like mostly harmless entertainment at first glance. A report from Infoblox urges the security community to pay closer attention to these websites, because some double as command-and-control (C2) infrastructure for espionage and malware distribution. Zach Edwards, staff threat researcher at Infoblox, suggests security researchers and the media have ignored these sites because the story is complicated and confusing. Infoblox says it tracks about 1.7 million Chinese-language casino websites that facilitate illegal gambling. These support North Korean money laundering and tax avoidance, among other dubious activities. And these casino sites can be difficult to distinguish from one another. They tend to use variations of common templates in terms of design and function. Many operate like a legal casino would, just relying on the advantage of house odds to profit. While these sites provide illegal gambling and adult entertainment for online visitors from China and Asia, some rely on US cloud providers for computing infrastructure. "Major US hosting companies (Amazon, Microsoft, Cloudflare, and Google) continue to host infrastructure associated with these domains," the Infoblox report explains. "One likely explanation is account theft at those providers, a practice documented previously as 'infrastructure laundering.'" That refers to hosting companies like Funnull that have reportedly rented IP addresses from Amazon Web Services and Microsoft and made those resources available to clients carrying out illegal activities. According to a July 2026 report from the UN Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC), disparate crime syndicates increasingly use common infrastructure for cybercrime, while online scams resulted in estimated losses of between $88.3 billion and $114.1 billion in 2025 across East Asia, Southeast Asia, Australia, and New Zealand. A subset of casino sites offer scam gambling, or "scambling." Visitors place bets but can't get their money out if they win. And then there's a subset of sites used by China-aligned threat groups. "China-aligned APT groups have been running the PeckBirdy framework since 2023, hiding their malware C2 domains inside low-quality Chinese-language casino websites," Infoblox said. PeckBirdy, as noted by Trend Micro researchers in January, is a script-based framework that attackers can load through compromised websites. In one campaign, attackers injected scripts into gambling sites that loaded PeckBirdy and displayed fake software update pages designed to entice victims to download malware. The problem is that each of these three types of sites, though they change frequently, looks similar. Infoblox notes that just over 3 percent of its enterprise customers resolved at least one PeckBirdy C2 domain. "The most important thing for defenders to do is stop ignoring casino domains," Infoblox argues. "An alert on a Chinese-language casino or adult domain that gets closed as an employee browsing violation is precisely the outcome the PeckBirdy operators are counting on. The decoy works because the dismissal is reasonable – these domains genuinely are, most of the time, exactly what they appear to be." Security analysts who review suspicious network contacts are advised to check whether these casino domains include malicious payloads before closing the review ticket. ® Intel spin-off Cornelis and newcomer Delos Data pitch open alternatives for scaling AI beyond the rack PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Pizza Bot runs locally and lets users interact with AI agents in an email-like interface. Iranian spies hit Windows machines with Chosen Brick data-stealing malware
2026-09-15

Iranian spies hit Windows machines with Chosen Brick data-stealing malware

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Iranian state cyber actors are targeting individuals using social messaging apps to deploy surveillance and data-stealing malware on their Windows machines, three Western governments warned. In all observed cases, Chosen Brick has infected Windows systems exclusively. Iran has used it since at least 2025 to take over individuals’ devices, stealing their contacts, emails, and social media messages, which allows the spies to track people’s movements, the FBI, UK National Cyber Security Centre, and the Netherlands’ General Intelligence and Security Service (AIVD) said on Tuesday. “Iran almost certainly uses cyber activity to support the repression of individuals who are seen as a threat to the regime, such as dissidents, activists and journalists,” the security advisory said. “In some cases, the Iranian intelligence services have plotted to kidnap or conduct lethal operations against individuals internationally, who they perceive as enemies of the regime.” These attacks typically begin with WhatsApp and Telegram messages, purportedly coming from individuals and organizations that the victim knows and trusts. The Iranian spies do a significant amount of research to prepare for these social engineering campaigns. By the time they send the initial message via a social media app, they have “extensive” knowledge of the targeted individual, their contacts, and relevant industry organizations to make the phony messages more believable, according to the agencies. After building rapport with the mark, the attackers convince them to download and open a file that appears to be a legitimate application. Specifically: Pictory, RunwayML, Norton Antivirus, Telegram, Adobe Flash Player, and KeePass are among the legitimate applications the malicious files have been made to resemble, the government agencies said. Upon opening the file, the malware executes without the victim’s knowledge, and will survive a reboot of the target device. Chosen Brick also adds exclusions to Microsoft Defender antivirus in an attempt to evade detection, and then connects to Telegram for command-and-control (C2) communications using a victim-specific Telegram bot. While the malware hasn’t yet been observed to automate lateral movement across the network, this is “technically possible,” the advisory noted. It does, however, download additional malware and set up persistence for new payloads on infected devices, using the same registry key that Chosen Brick uses to establish its own persistence on a Windows device: HKCU\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run. Other features include enumerating running processes and system information, capturing screen and audio content, stealing emails, along with Telegram and WhatsApp data from web browsers, and wiping the computer system. “Organizations that are concerned Chosen Brick has been executed should contact their IT providers, either internal or external, to investigate,” the US, UK, and the Netherlands warned. “As this actor targets personal devices, not just corporate devices, organizations are recommended to circulate this with their staff that are likely to be targeted and support them in checking their personal devices too.” The Western agencies’ latest Iran alert follows a series of water and energy cyberattacks that researchers and media reports have linked to Iran, although the US and UK governments have stopped short of formally attributing them, as the military conflict between Iran and the US approaches its seventh month. In August, America’s lead cybersecurity agency, CISA, disclosed that the July cyberattacks that disrupted American water utilities across 12 states targeted more than 100 internet-exposed water systems. CISA did not, however, attribute the campaign to Iran or anyone else. Around the same time, a suspected Iran-linked cyberattack also shut down a small UK power plant. Also in August, five US agencies warned that attackers are using AI-generated exploitation scripts to break into internet-exposed Siemens S7 Series programmable logic controllers (PLCs) at water, manufacturing, energy, and other critical facilities. “This is not a theoretical risk – it is an active threat,” the feds warned.  ® Intel spin-off Cornelis and newcomer Delos Data pitch open alternatives for scaling AI beyond the rack PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Pizza Bot runs locally and lets users interact with AI agents in an email-like interface. Higher-enriched uranium for datacenters has DoE all aglow
2026-09-15

Higher-enriched uranium for datacenters has DoE all aglow

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
HALEU is still being produced at a snail's pace; Nusano says its federal backing could speed things up – eventually The US Department of Energy has selected a new participant for a program aimed at expanding the domestic supply of fuel for next-generation nuclear reactors. However, we shouldn't expect that to mean the US' ever-growing fleet of datacenters will be powered by low-carbon nuclear energy any time soon. Utah-based Nusano announced on Monday that the DoE had selected it for its Nuclear Energy Launch Pad Program designed to help companies commercialize their nuclear power concepts. In this case, Nusano is working on a new method of creating high-assay low-enriched uranium, or HALEU, fuel that’s required for most next-generation nuclear reactors, like small modular reactors (SMRs) and molten salt reactors. HALEU is defined as uranium enay, low-riched to between 5 and 20 weight percent of U-235, the primary fissile isotope that nuclear reactors use to generate power. Traditional nuclear reactors use low-enriched uranium of under five percent. The higher enrichment is needed for smaller, more efficient reactor designs that are currently under development. Several advanced reactor demonstrations in the US have reached zero-power criticality, but none is yet operating commercially. The US has been without a steady commercial-scale supply of HALEU for years, with Russia historically the main commercial supplier and China also possessing HALEU enrichment capability. As of 2026, there’s only a single company in the US actively producing HALEU, and it’s not Nusano. The DoE has signed agreements with several others to produce more HALEU and related fuel products, but those firms are still establishing their facilities, and production remains minuscule. Centrus, the only American company producing HALEU, has generated less than two metric tons since it signed a contract with the government in 2019. Nusano expects to blow Centrus’ meager fuel generation out of the water with its HALEU enrichment design. That said, the company’s design hasn’t actually been realized yet. Nusano didn’t answer a question about whether it had built a functional demonstration HALEU enrichment unit, and when asked whether it had produced any HALEU, the company said that prototype development is still under way. “Nusano expects each unit will produce 5.9 metric tons (MT) of HALEU per year, with the first unit scheduled to be operational in 2031,” Nusano comms boss Scott Larrivee told The Register in an email. “Nusano’s Launch Pad application includes a well-developed, practical plan to move from proof-of-concept to commercial implementation.” As for what that’ll look like, Nusano describes a method that’s completely different from other forms of HALEU production. Whereas other HALEU refiners use gas centrifuges to produce HALEU, spinning uranium hexafluoride gas at high speeds to increase U-235 concentration, Nusano’s method ditches gas entirely. “The method is complex, costly, and potentially hazardous,” Larrivee explained. “If released, uranium hexafluoride reacts with moisture in air or human tissue to form highly toxic, corrosive hydrogen fluoride and uranyl fluoride.” Those gases, Larrivee said, can cause potentially fatal injuries like chemical burns and lung and kidney damage. Nusano is proposing a method that uses uranium feedstock that’s converted to metal prior to enrichment. Unspecified “proprietary separation techniques” are then used to enrich the metal and separate out U-235. The HALEU output of Nusano’s design is metal. “The Nusano process is metal in, metal out. No deconversion. No gaseous fluorine chemistry anywhere,” Larrivee added. Nusano’s current design only requires 1,200 square feet per HALEU unit, and the company anticipates its production cost will be lower than those of gas-centrifuge enrichment. That said, a lot of Nusano units will be needed to meet expected American demand. According to the DoE, HALEU demand could reach 50 metric tons per year by 2035, and Nusano is only expecting to get its first 5.9 MT/year unit up and running by 2031. It’s also not clear if Nusano’s current promises will still be the same if and when its HALEU units are up and running. In June 2025, the company said it had achieved a breakthrough that would allow a single HALEU unit to produce 50 metric tons annually and enable it to scale production to 350 metric tons per year by 2029. Search the company website for that announcement today, and it’s gone. We were only able to see it by pulling up an archived copy. Larrivee said that announcement was out of date, developed under prior company leadership, and that the numbers in the latest release were reflective of current development plans. A number of datacenter operators have promised to embrace SMRs and other next-gen nuclear when the technology is available. With HALEU output still low and commercial-scale production years away, it's unclear when those promises will be realized. Meanwhile, the Trump administration's Environmental Protection Agency has just scrapped Biden-era emissions regulations for coal and gas power plants, which it said means coal generation in the US could increase by more than ten times current levels. In other words, next-gen nuclear is, as ever, still on the horizon while bit barns crank up the fossil fuel turbines, now with even less regard to environmental impacts. ® Intel spin-off Cornelis and newcomer Delos Data pitch open alternatives for scaling AI beyond the rack PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Pizza Bot runs locally and lets users interact with AI agents in an email-like interface. America is building datacenters faster than the grid can power them
2026-09-15

America is building datacenters faster than the grid can power them

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Meeting expected energy consumption through 2030 will require $110 billion in new generation resources Financial service biz Moody's says the availability of power is still a major constraint on US datacenter growth as the pace of construction exceeds the speed at which the nation's grid infrastructure can add the requisite capacity. America is the epicenter of the datacenter construction boom, driven by the AI craze and demand for compute capacity to train ever-larger foundation models. The latest report from Moody's estimates the power needed by all those bit barns will reach 426 terawatt-hours (TWh) in 2030, citing data from the International Energy Agency (IEA). That is almost double the 2025 figure. It falls on the power sector to develop new resources to meet this extra demand as traditional power generation is struggling to keep pace. Moody's calculates that building the additional generation capacity will cost about $110 billion, adding an estimated $25 billion to $30 billion a year to electricity system costs. Yet efforts to supply the rapidly growing number of cloud and AI server farms are running up against lengthy permitting processes and long lead times for key equipment and materials, causing delays of up to seven years in some cases. Management consultancy Bain & Company highlighted the problem nearly two years ago when it warned that energy companies needed to significantly boost their generation capacity, reversing years of flat or declining demand. Transmission buildout is a parallel bottleneck, with $80 billion to $115 billion in grid investment through 2030 already approved or under construction across US power markets, Moody's says, with the potential to triple over the next decade. Investment bank Jefferies reported in June that half of the extra US datacenter capacity planned for 2026 is unlikely to come online this year, blaming power availability and grid connection setbacks, along with other issues including zoning and permitting challenges. Regions able to accelerate development, such as Texas, have experienced a wave of datacenter construction. The resulting pressure on local grids has prompted some authorities to clamp down on new connections for compute sheds. Moody's believes most new IT campus operators are still likely to seek grid connections to limit development costs and benefit from service reliability and shared redundancy. Yet many are also seeking alternatives, including on-site behind-the-meter (BTM) power generation to avoid delays in bringing facilities online. The document also notes that the growing energy demands from server farms are inflating power prices and customer bills in some regions of America, as The Register reported earlier this year. This has led to affordability concerns, fueling opposition to datacenters and prompting measures such as stricter approval frameworks and moratoriums on new projects. President Trump even introduced a Ratepayer Protection Pledge, under which tech giants and energy companies committed to shielding consumers from rising energy costs due to the datacenter boom. In the long term, Moody's believes that large-scale infrastructure investment will ultimately strengthen grid reliability and affordability, despite the risks. Extra generation capacity will alleviate longstanding supply constraints, it says, reducing price volatility during periods of extreme weather or high demand, while expanded transmission networks could improve access to lower-cost generation resources, particularly when datacenter developers or their tenants help finance the associated infrastructure. ® Intel spin-off Cornelis and newcomer Delos Data pitch open alternatives for scaling AI beyond the rack PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Pizza Bot runs locally and lets users interact with AI agents in an email-like interface. Cisco email security boxes can be rooted by... an email
2026-09-15

Cisco email security boxes can be rooted by... an email

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Attackers already exploiting the critical flaw, and Cisco warns they may be able to cover their tracks once they're in Criminals are exploiting a critical Cisco Secure Email Gateway flaw that can turn a malicious email into root access. The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-76461, carries a 9.8 CVSS score and affects physical and virtual Secure Email Gateway appliances regardless of their configuration. Cisco says there are no workarounds, so patching is the only fix. The bug lies in how Cisco's AsyncOS software handles incoming email. An attacker doesn't need to log in: they can send a booby-trapped message through a vulnerable gateway and, if the exploit works, run commands as root. Which is not exactly what you want from the box tasked with keeping nasty emails out. Cisco's Product Security Incident Response Team said it became aware of active exploitation in September, although the networking giant hasn't said who is behind the attacks, how long they have been going on, or how many organizations have been compromised. Cisco uncovered the bug while resolving a Technical Assistance Center support case. Signs suggest at least some Cisco cloud customers were caught up in the attacks. Cisco said it investigated devices belonging to its Secure Email Cloud service and directly contacted customers whose appliances showed indicators of possible compromise. It is now carrying out remediation and recovery work and says all of its Secure Email Cloud devices have been upgraded to AsyncOS 16.5.0-780. Admins running their own appliances have a little more work to do. Cisco recommends checking logs for signs of suspicious activity, but warns that finding nothing doesn't necessarily mean the system is clean. Once attackers have root access, Cisco says they could tamper with the logs and cover their tracks. Admins are also being told to check network and firewall logs for anything unusual, rather than relying on the gateway itself for answers. For virtual appliances suspected of being compromised, Cisco's recovery advice is fairly drastic: preserve the forensic evidence, deploy a fresh VM running fixed software, rebuild the configuration, and rotate credentials and cryptographic material. Cisco has fixed the bug in AsyncOS releases 15.5.5-014, 16.0.4-302 and 16.5.0-780, with customers strongly encouraged to move to the latter. There's still a decent-sized target pool out there. The Shadowserver Foundation was tracking more than 400 Cisco Secure Email Gateway appliances exposed to the internet as of Monday The flaw has also landed in CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog, with US federal civilian agencies ordered to remediate it by September 17. CVE-2026-76461 comes less than a year after attackers exploited another critical AsyncOS flaw, CVE-2025-20393, to break into Cisco Secure Email Gateway appliances and install persistence mechanisms. That bug eventually scored a perfect 10. For anyone still running an affected gateway, the message is fairly simple: the box designed to inspect hostile email can itself be pwned by one; attackers are already doing it, and there is no workaround to hide behind.  ® Intel spin-off Cornelis and newcomer Delos Data pitch open alternatives for scaling AI beyond the rack PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Pizza Bot runs locally and lets users interact with AI agents in an email-like interface. What if social media isn’t hurting kids?

The Verge | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
A new book says modern schooling, not phones, are behind youth mental health declines. Will anyone listen? A new book says modern schooling, not phones, are behind youth mental health declines. Will anyone listen? Two years ago, social psychologist Jonathan Haidt released his New York Times bestseller The Anxious Generation. The book posits that the rise of social media and smartphone use is largely to blame for the prominent decline in teen mental health since 2010. It quickly became a central rallying cry of the social media backlash. Haidt has been credited with helping inspire Australia’s nationwide teen social media ban. He has joined forces with bereaved parents who are fighting tech companies in court. His name comes up unprompted in conversations with people in power who say his book opened their eyes to an online crisis. Haidt’s theory has received regular critiques from recognized academics. Candice Odgers, a leading adolescent mental health researcher, wrote a scathing review of Haidt’s analysis in Nature that opened by conceding, “this book is going to sell a lot of copies, because Jonathan Haidt is telling a scary story about children’s development that many parents are primed to believe.” Now, in the wake of that highly accurate prediction, a former colleague of Haidt’s seeks to not only take down his theory, but also offer an alternative. The biggest question may not be whether he’s correct — but whether it’s too late to change course if he is. In his new book Restoring Childhood, psychology researcher Peter Gray argues that teens’ mental health declined thanks to decades of steadily reduced autonomy, and after a brief period of reprieve, fell again in the 2010s not — as Haidt argues — because of phones and social media, but because of rigid schooling methods linked to the Common Core curriculum and government pressure to improve children’s performance. And far from being damaging, Gray also argues the adoption of home internet in the 1990s and 2000s actually drove a rebound in teens’ mental health by restoring freedom and connection lost in earlier decades. He fears social media bans aren’t just — as Odgers has argued — a distraction from real solutions, but a new harm in themselves. “You take a smartphone away from your kids and that study suggests you’ll make them less happy” In an interview on The Vergecast, Gray told me that Haidt’s book “completely ignored” dozens of meta-analyses and other research that’s found “not even a meaningful correlation between the amount of time kids are on social media or on smartphones and their mental health.” Where there is some connection, Gray adds, studies suggest that teens may use social media to cope with their depression or anxiety. He references a study in Florida that found 11-to-13-year-olds who owned smartphones were more likely to spend real-world time with friends. “You take a smartphone away from your kids and that study suggests you’ll make them less happy and they’ll have less friends,” he says. Gray says that in studies through the years, kids consistently identify school pressure, not social media, as their top source of anxiety. “That doesn’t mean it’s the primary source for everybody, but when you’re looking at the population at large, every single study that I’ve been able to find where kids are asked, ‘What’s the primary source of your anxiety or your stress or your depression?’, in every single one of them, school pressure comes out on top,” Gray tells me. “Nothing else comes close.” Gray and Haidt both believe that since at least the 1980s, increased restrictions on kids’ independence and unstructured play have damaged their resilience. While it used to be common for kids to play together unsupervised, that practice has diminished in part due to the rise of “stranger danger” narratives fostered by rare, but high-profile, crimes. In 2017 the pair helped cofound an organization called Let Grow, which advocates for policies that make it easier for parents to let their children play freely, like ensuring they can’t be considered neglectful for letting kids outside by themselves. But the two have split sharply over the impact of the internet. Gray’s book is intended as a deliberate rebuttal to Haidt’s — after The Anxious Generation’s release, Gray resigned from the board of Let Grow to avoid conflict within the organization. Haidt did not respond to a request for comment from The Verge, but he told The Atlantic’s Kaitlyn Tiffany in a July article that the 1990s and early 2000s dip in teen suicide rates may be credited to a phase-out of leaded gasoline, since lead exposure has been linked to mental health issues. He also told Tiffany that Gray relied too heavily on a minority dissenting group of researchers. Tiffany writes that many of the more than a dozen tech and child development researchers she spoke to “expressed concern that Haidt overstates the strength of correlational findings and suggests causation where it hasn’t been proved.” Gray accuses Haidt of stirring a moral panic over technology that isn’t, on the whole, harmful. Looking across research, he says, Common Core is the clearest explanation for the sudden decline in teen mental health. And at the population level, the impact of social media “balances out. Some kids are doing better, some kids are doing worse because they’re on social media.” “We need to stop treating kids as if they’re idiots” Gray says he’s not opposed to all guardrails, including school phone bans and the concept of age verification for accessing pornography. But he questions the effectiveness of bans on social media, knowing kids are likely to get around them, and is hesitant to restrict platforms that let kids express themselves — suggesting instead that parents focus on teaching their children to navigate them. “We need to stop treating kids as if they’re idiots,” he told me. “We need to start understanding that kids are pretty good at knowing what’s good for them and not good for them and they can benefit from our help in learning how to do these things.” But increasingly, parents of younger teens may not have that choice. Haidt’s theory holds undisputed dominance in many circles, including among politicians. Since Australia’s social media ban for teens, the UK and European Union are among those considering their own bans or platform limits. In the US, states like California and New York have enacted limits on certain social media features, and a federal proposal would restrict algorithmic feeds for kids under 17. Even among people who find Gray’s arguments about Common Core convincing, some might still see taking kids offline as a harmless precaution. With so much momentum behind the backlash against phones and social media, can Gray’s book still make a difference? “I certainly hope it’s not too late,” he says. He points out that some moral panics have petered out. In the 1990s and 2000s, for instance, many people worried that video games like Mortal Kombat and Doom were making kids more violent, connecting their rise to that of school shootings. Eventually, research showed many mass shooters didn’t play video games after all. Attempts at regulation — including a California ban on the sale of violent games to children — were largely overruled. And many kids who did play them grew up to become those in charge. “The science won out eventually,” Gray says. “So it’s not me that’s going to win out. It’s the science that’s going to win out.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.

Kia’s electric van lineup is getting more interesting with reveal of PV7
